# Chapter 24: Adrenal Cortex - Block 3: Adrenal Insufficiency (Primary Addison Disease vs Secondary Pituitary vs Tertiary Hypothalamic; Post-Glucocorticoid Withdrawal, Waterhouse-Friderichsen Syndrome), Dynamic Testing (Cosyntropin Stimulation Test, Metyrapone Test, CRH Test), Hypercortisolism / Cushing Syndrome (Exogenous Steroids, Cushing Disease, Ectopic ACTH [SCLC, Carcinoid], Adrenal Adenoma/Carcinoma), Diagnostic Algorithms (Low-Dose & High-Dose Dexamethasone, 24-hr UFC, Midnight Salivary Cortisol), Glucocorticoid Resistance, Nelson Syndrome

### Question 101: Screening for Cushing Syndrome: The Three First-Line Diagnostic Modalities
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The diagnostic criteria and interpretation of first-line screening tests for Cushing syndrome, specifically the 1-mg overnight low-dose dexamethasone suppression test (LDDST) cutoff for normal suppression (库欣综合征筛查的三项一线检测及低剂量地塞米松抑制试验的正常抑制阈值).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 36-year-old woman presents with progressive central weight gain (BMI 34 kg/m^2), facial roundness and plethora ('moon facies'), hirsutism, wide violaceous abdominal striae, and newly diagnosed hypertension (BP 154/96 mmHg). She denies any use of oral, inhaled, topical, or injected glucocorticoids. Her physician suspects Cushing syndrome. To screen for endogenous hypercortisolemia, the physician orders three first-line tests: a 24-hour urinary free cortisol (24h UFC), a late-night salivary cortisol (LNSC), and a 1-mg overnight low-dose dexamethasone suppression test (LDDST). The 24h UFC is elevated at 185 mcg/day (normal < 50 mcg/day), and the LNSC is elevated at 0.22 mcg/dL on two separate nights (normal < 0.10 mcg/dL). The 1-mg LDDST is performed, with dexamethasone administered at 11:00 PM, and serum cortisol is measured at 8:00 AM the following morning. The result is 2.1 mcg/dL. What serum cortisol cutoff at 8:00 AM following a 1-mg overnight dexamethasone suppression test defines normal physiological suppression?

(A) < 0.5 mcg/dL (14 nmol/L)
(B) < 1.0 mcg/dL (28 nmol/L)
(C) < 1.5 mcg/dL (41 nmol/L)
(D) < 1.8 mcg/dL (50 nmol/L)
(E) < 2.0 mcg/dL (55 nmol/L)

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- The patient presents with classic signs and symptoms of Cushing syndrome (central obesity, moon facies, hirsutism, striae, hypertension).
- Exogenous glucocorticoid use is ruled out.
- The physician correctly orders three first-line screening tests for Cushing syndrome according to Endocrine Society guidelines.
- The 24h UFC and LNSC results are elevated, strongly suggesting hypercortisolemia.
- The question specifically asks for the cutoff value for *normal* suppression in the 1-mg overnight LDDST.
- The patient's actual LDDST result (2.1 mcg/dL) is *above* the normal suppression cutoff, consistent with the elevated screening tests and clinical suspicion of Cushing syndrome. The question, however, asks for the *definition* of normal suppression, not the interpretation of this specific patient's result.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺皮质生理机制与分子生物学推导)
- **HPA Axis Regulation:** The hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal (HPA) axis regulates cortisol production. CRH from the hypothalamus stimulates ACTH release from the anterior pituitary, which in turn stimulates cortisol synthesis and release from the adrenal cortex (zona fasciculata).
- **Negative Feedback:** Cortisol exerts negative feedback on both the hypothalamus (reducing CRH) and the pituitary (reducing ACTH).
- **Dexamethasone Suppression Test:** Dexamethasone is a potent synthetic glucocorticoid that mimics cortisol's negative feedback effect. In healthy individuals, administration of dexamethasone suppresses ACTH release, leading to decreased cortisol production by the adrenal glands.
- **Low-Dose Dexamethasone Suppression Test (LDDST):** The 1-mg overnight LDDST tests the integrity of the negative feedback loop. A normal HPA axis will respond to the exogenous dexamethasone by suppressing ACTH and subsequently cortisol production.
- **Cutoff Value:** The established cutoff for normal suppression in the 1-mg overnight LDDST is a serum cortisol level less than 1.8 mcg/dL (50 nmol/L) at 8:00 AM. Failure to suppress (cortisol ≥ 1.8 mcg/dL) indicates autonomous cortisol production or impaired feedback sensitivity, characteristic of Cushing syndrome. This cutoff was determined based on studies in healthy volunteers.
- **Steroidogenesis:** Cortisol synthesis occurs in the adrenal cortex via a multi-step enzymatic pathway starting from cholesterol. ACTH stimulates the initial step (cholesterol transport into mitochondria via StAR protein and cleavage by CYP11A1) and subsequent steps involving CYP21A2, CYP11B1, and CYP17A1. Dexamethasone suppresses ACTH, thereby reducing the drive for cortisol synthesis.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient presents with clinical features suggestive of Cushing syndrome.
2. The physician orders appropriate first-line screening tests.
3. The question asks specifically about the interpretation criteria for one of these tests: the 1-mg overnight LDDST.
4. The LDDST assesses the HPA axis's ability to suppress cortisol production in response to exogenous glucocorticoid (dexamethasone).
5. A normal response is defined by a serum cortisol level below a specific threshold at 8:00 AM.
6. Recall or look up the standard cutoff value for normal suppression in the 1-mg overnight LDDST.
7. The established cutoff is < 1.8 mcg/dL (50 nmol/L).
8. Compare this value to the options provided. Option (D) matches the standard cutoff.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) < 0.5 mcg/dL (14 nmol/L):** This value is too low. While some individuals might suppress below this level, it is not the standard cutoff used to define normal suppression in the 1-mg overnight LDDST. This cutoff might be more relevant for the 2-day LDDST (2mg/day for 48 hours).
- **(B) < 1.0 mcg/dL (28 nmol/L):** This value is also too low for the standard 1-mg overnight LDDST cutoff. It represents a more profound level of suppression than typically required to define a normal response.
- **(C) < 1.5 mcg/dL (41 nmol/L):** This value is closer but still below the widely accepted standard cutoff of 1.8 mcg/dL. It might be used in some specific contexts or older guidelines, but 1.8 mcg/dL is the most commonly cited and accepted value based on current Endocrine Society guidelines and large studies.
- **(D) < 1.8 mcg/dL (50 nmol/L):** This is the correct cutoff value widely accepted and recommended by the Endocrine Society for defining normal suppression in the 1-mg overnight LDDST. Failure to suppress (≥ 1.8 mcg/dL) is considered abnormal and suggestive of Cushing syndrome.
- **(E) < 2.0 mcg/dL (55 nmol/L):** This value is slightly higher than the standard cutoff. A cortisol level of 2.0 mcg/dL would be considered abnormal (failure to suppress) according to current guidelines.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Test for Cushing Syndrome | Principle                                     | Normal Result / Interpretation                                  | Abnormal Result / Interpretation                               |
| :------------------------ | :-------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------ |
| 24h UFC                   | Measures total free cortisol excretion         | < 50 mcg/day (or < 90 nmol/day)                               | > 3x ULN (e.g., > 150 mcg/day) confirms hypercortisolemia     |
| LNSC                      | Measures cortisol in saliva at night           | < 0.10 mcg/dL (4 nmol/L) on 2 separate nights                   | > 0.10 mcg/dL (4 nmol/L) on 2 separate nights suggests Cushing |
| 1-mg LDDST                | Assesses HPA axis feedback suppression        | Serum cortisol < 1.8 mcg/dL (50 nmol/L) at 8:00 AM             | Serum cortisol ≥ 1.8 mcg/dL (50 nmol/L) suggests Cushing     |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
The 1-mg overnight low-dose dexamethasone suppression test is a key first-line screening tool for Cushing syndrome. Normal physiological suppression is defined as a serum cortisol level below 1.8 mcg/dL (50 nmol/L) at 8:00 AM the following morning. Failure to suppress above this threshold indicates pathological hypercortisolemia and warrants further investigation. Remember the three first-line tests: 24h UFC, LNSC, and 1-mg LDDST.

### Question 102: Adrenal Cortex Physiological Mechanism
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The differential diagnostic utility of low-dose versus high-dose dexamethasone suppression tests in evaluating hypercortisolism (Cushing syndrome), specifically distinguishing between pituitary-dependent (Cushing disease) and ectopic ACTH secretion.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old female presents with complaints of weight gain (primarily truncal), facial plethora, easy bruising, and muscle weakness over the past year. Physical examination reveals central obesity, a buffalo hump, purple striae on the abdomen, and hypertension (150/90 mmHg). Initial laboratory investigations show hyperglycemia (fasting glucose 115 mg/dL) and hypokalemia (serum potassium 3.2 mEq/L). A 24-hour urinary free cortisol level is elevated at 180 mcg/day (normal < 50 mcg/day), confirming hypercortisolism. To further evaluate the cause, a 1-mg overnight low-dose dexamethasone suppression test was performed. The following morning, her serum cortisol level was 8.5 mcg/dL (normal suppression < 1.8 mcg/dL), indicating that cortisol secretion is not suppressed by low-dose dexamethasone. The next step in the diagnostic workup is to differentiate between the possible causes of ACTH-dependent Cushing syndrome. Which of the following is the primary indication for performing an 8-mg high-dose dexamethasone suppression test?

(A) To screen for the presence of Cushing syndrome in a patient with suggestive clinical features but normal 24-hour urinary free cortisol.
(B) To differentiate between pituitary Cushing disease and adrenal Cushing syndrome (adenoma or carcinoma).
(C) To assess the adrenal glands' responsiveness to ACTH stimulation in cases of suspected adrenal insufficiency.
(D) To confirm the diagnosis of Cushing syndrome after a positive low-dose dexamethasone suppression test.
(E) To differentiate between pituitary Cushing disease and ectopic ACTH secretion.

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 45-year-old female with classic signs/symptoms of Cushing syndrome (weight gain, facial plethora, bruising, muscle weakness, central obesity, buffalo hump, purple striae, hypertension).
- **Laboratory Findings:** Hyperglycemia, hypokalemia, elevated 24-hour urinary free cortisol (confirms hypercortisolism).
- **Low-Dose Dexamethasone Suppression Test (LDDST):** Failure to suppress serum cortisol (8.5 mcg/dL) confirms Cushing syndrome and indicates ACTH-dependent hypercortisolism (as both ACTH-dependent and ACTH-independent causes fail to suppress with 1 mg dexamethasone).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the primary indication of the *high-dose* dexamethasone suppression test (HDDST) *after* confirming Cushing syndrome and establishing ACTH-dependence via failure to suppress on LDDST.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺皮质生理机制与分子生物学推导)
- **Cushing Syndrome Etiologies:**
    - ACTH-dependent: Pituitary adenoma (Cushing disease, ~70%), Ectopic ACTH production (e.g., SCLC, ~15-20%), Ectopic CRH production (rare).
    - ACTH-independent: Adrenal adenoma (~10%), Adrenal carcinoma (~5%), Exogenous glucocorticoid use (most common overall cause).
- **Dexamethasone Suppression Tests:**
    - **Low-Dose (1 mg):** Mimics physiological cortisol levels. In normal individuals, it suppresses ACTH release from the pituitary via negative feedback, leading to decreased adrenal cortisol production. In *all* forms of Cushing syndrome (pituitary, ectopic, adrenal), the cortisol production is autonomous or ACTH-driven and *fails* to suppress. Therefore, LDDST is primarily used to *confirm* the diagnosis of Cushing syndrome.
    - **High-Dose (8 mg):** Provides a large pharmacological dose of glucocorticoid.
        - **Pituitary Adenomas (Cushing Disease):** These tumors retain some degree of negative feedback sensitivity, albeit blunted. The high dose of dexamethasone can partially suppress ACTH secretion from the pituitary adenoma, leading to a significant (>50%) reduction in serum cortisol levels.
        - **Ectopic ACTH Tumors:** These tumors (e.g., SCLC) are typically completely autonomous and insensitive to negative feedback. They continue to secrete ACTH regardless of the dexamethasone dose, resulting in *no significant suppression* (<50% suppression) of cortisol.
        - **Adrenal Tumors:** These tumors produce cortisol independently of ACTH and are therefore also insensitive to dexamethasone suppression.
- **Diagnostic Algorithm:**
    1. Confirm hypercortisolism (e.g., elevated 24-hr UFC, late-night salivary cortisol).
    2. Establish ACTH-dependence (measure plasma ACTH; if elevated or inappropriately normal, it's ACTH-dependent; if suppressed, it's ACTH-independent).
    3. If ACTH-dependent, perform LDDST. Failure to suppress confirms Cushing syndrome.
    4. If LDDST fails to suppress, perform HDDST to differentiate between Cushing disease (pituitary) and ectopic ACTH secretion. Suppression (>50%) points to Cushing disease; lack of suppression (<50%) points to ectopic ACTH or adrenal tumor (though adrenal tumor would have low ACTH).

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient presents with classic signs and symptoms of Cushing syndrome.
2. Elevated 24-hour urinary free cortisol confirms hypercortisolism.
3. The LDDST fails, confirming Cushing syndrome and indicating ACTH-dependence (since both ACTH-dependent and independent causes fail to suppress on LDDST).
4. The question asks for the purpose of the *next* step, the HDDST.
5. The HDDST is used to differentiate between the two main causes of ACTH-dependent Cushing syndrome: pituitary adenoma (Cushing disease) and ectopic ACTH production. Pituitary adenomas usually show some suppression with high-dose dexamethasone, while ectopic sources typically do not.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) To screen for the presence of Cushing syndrome in a patient with suggestive clinical features but normal 24-hour urinary free cortisol.** Incorrect. The LDDST (or other tests like late-night salivary cortisol) is used for screening, not the HDDST. Furthermore, the vignette states the 24-hr UFC is *elevated*, confirming hypercortisolism.
- **(B) To differentiate between pituitary Cushing disease and adrenal Cushing syndrome (adenoma or carcinoma).** Incorrect. While the HDDST can help distinguish ACTH-dependent causes, it does not reliably differentiate between pituitary and adrenal causes. Adrenal tumors are ACTH-independent (low ACTH) and also fail to suppress on HDDST, but the primary distinction between pituitary and adrenal is made by measuring plasma ACTH levels *before* suppression testing.
- **(C) To assess the adrenal glands' responsiveness to ACTH stimulation in cases of suspected adrenal insufficiency.** Incorrect. This describes the Cosyntropin (synthetic ACTH) stimulation test, used to diagnose primary adrenal insufficiency (Addison disease). Dexamethasone is a synthetic glucocorticoid, not ACTH.
- **(D) To confirm the diagnosis of Cushing syndrome after a positive low-dose dexamethasone suppression test.** Incorrect. The LDDST is used to confirm Cushing syndrome, not the HDDST. The HDDST is performed *after* Cushing syndrome is confirmed by LDDST (or other tests) to determine the etiology.
- **(E) To differentiate between pituitary Cushing disease and ectopic ACTH secretion.** Correct. This is the specific purpose of the HDDST in patients with confirmed ACTH-dependent Cushing syndrome (failure to suppress on LDDST).

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Test                                  | Dose          | Primary Purpose                                                                 | Suppression Pattern (Cushing Disease) | Suppression Pattern (Ectopic ACTH) | Suppression Pattern (Adrenal Tumor) |
| :------------------------------------ | :------------ | :----------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :--------------------------------- |
| Low-Dose Dexamethasone Suppression Test | 1 mg          | Confirm Cushing Syndrome                                                        | No Suppression                      | No Suppression                   | No Suppression                    |
| High-Dose Dexamethasone Suppression Test | 8 mg          | Differentiate ACTH-dependent causes (Pituitary vs. Ectopic)                     | Significant (>50%) Suppression      | No Significant (<50%) Suppression | No Suppression                    |
| Cosyntropin Stimulation Test          | 250 mcg IV    | Diagnose Primary Adrenal Insufficiency                                         | N/A                                 | N/A                               | Increased Cortisol                 |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
The low-dose dexamethasone suppression test confirms the presence of Cushing syndrome by demonstrating a lack of cortisol suppression. The high-dose dexamethasone suppression test is then used specifically in patients with ACTH-dependent Cushing syndrome to differentiate between a pituitary source (Cushing disease), which typically shows partial suppression, and an ectopic source of ACTH, which typically does not suppress.

### Question 103: Adrenal Cortex Physiological Mechanism
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal (HPA) axis feedback regulation and the interpretation of plasma ACTH levels in the context of hypercortisolemia to differentiate between ACTH-dependent and ACTH-independent Cushing syndrome. (下丘脑-垂体-肾上腺轴的负反馈调节以及在库欣综合征背景下解读血浆ACTH水平以区分ACTH依赖性和ACTH非依赖性库欣综合征)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 44-year-old woman presents with complaints of weight gain (particularly truncal obesity), easy bruising, purple striae on her abdomen, and muscle weakness over the past year. Her past medical history is unremarkable, and she denies exogenous steroid use. Physical examination reveals central obesity, a "buffalo hump," and thin skin. Laboratory investigations confirm hypercortisolemia: 24-hour urinary free cortisol (UFC) is elevated at 380 mcg/day (normal < 50 mcg/day), and she fails to suppress cortisol production after a 1-mg overnight dexamethasone suppression test, with a morning serum cortisol level of 14 mcg/dL (normal suppression < 1.8 mcg/dL). To determine the etiology of her hypercortisolemia, a baseline morning plasma adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH) level is measured and found to be undetectable, < 1.0 pg/mL (normal reference range: 10 to 60 pg/mL). Which anatomical structure is the most likely source of the excess cortisol production in this patient?

(A) Pituitary adenoma secreting ACTH
(B) Ectopic source secreting ACTH (e.g., small cell lung cancer)
(C) Adrenal cortex adenoma or carcinoma
(D) Hypothalamus secreting corticotropin-releasing hormone (CRH)
(E) Exogenous glucocorticoid administration

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 44-year-old woman with classic signs and symptoms of Cushing syndrome (weight gain, truncal obesity, easy bruising, purple striae, muscle weakness, buffalo hump, thin skin). This points towards hypercortisolemia.
- **Laboratory Confirmation of Hypercortisolemia:** Elevated 24-hour UFC (380 mcg/day) and failure to suppress cortisol after low-dose dexamethasone (morning cortisol 14 mcg/dL) confirm the diagnosis of Cushing syndrome.
- **Key Diagnostic Test:** Undetectable baseline morning plasma ACTH (< 1.0 pg/mL). This is the crucial piece of information for localizing the source.
- **Interpretation:** In the context of confirmed hypercortisolemia, a suppressed or undetectable plasma ACTH level indicates that the excess cortisol production is autonomous and independent of pituitary ACTH stimulation. The adrenal gland itself is the source of the problem, producing cortisol without appropriate regulation by the HPA axis.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺皮质生理机制与分子生物学推导)
- **HPA Axis Regulation:** The hypothalamus releases CRH, stimulating the pituitary to release ACTH. ACTH travels via the portal system to the adrenal cortex, stimulating steroidogenesis, primarily cortisol production in the zona fasciculata. Cortisol exerts negative feedback on both the hypothalamus (reducing CRH) and the pituitary (reducing ACTH).
- **Cushing Syndrome Etiology:** Cushing syndrome results from prolonged exposure to excess cortisol. The source can be endogenous (ACTH-dependent or ACTH-independent) or exogenous.
- **ACTH-Dependent Cushing:** Caused by excessive ACTH secretion, leading to bilateral adrenal hyperplasia and hypercortisolism. Sources include pituitary adenomas (Cushing disease, ~80% of endogenous cases) or ectopic ACTH production (e.g., SCLC, carcinoid tumors, ~20% of endogenous cases). In these cases, plasma ACTH levels are typically normal or elevated.
- **ACTH-Independent Cushing:** Caused by autonomous cortisol production from the adrenal cortex, independent of ACTH stimulation. This leads to suppression of hypothalamic CRH and pituitary ACTH secretion due to the potent negative feedback effect of high cortisol levels. Sources include adrenal adenomas, adrenal carcinomas, or rare bilateral adrenal hyperplasia (e.g., PPNAD, AIMAH). In these cases, plasma ACTH levels are typically suppressed (< 5 pg/mL, often undetectable).
- **Exogenous Glucocorticoids:** Administration of exogenous glucocorticoids also suppresses the HPA axis, leading to low ACTH and adrenal atrophy. This mimics ACTH-independent Cushing biochemically but is due to external steroid intake. The vignette explicitly states the patient denies exogenous steroid use.
- **The ACTH "Fork":** Measuring plasma ACTH is the critical first step in differentiating the cause of endogenous Cushing syndrome after hypercortisolemia is confirmed. Low ACTH (< 5 pg/mL) points to an adrenal source, while normal or high ACTH points to a pituitary or ectopic source.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient presents with clinical features suggestive of Cushing syndrome.
2. Laboratory tests (elevated 24h UFC, failure of low-dose dexamethasone suppression) confirm hypercortisolemia, establishing the diagnosis of Cushing syndrome.
3. The next step is to determine the etiology by measuring plasma ACTH.
4. The patient's plasma ACTH level is undetectable (< 1.0 pg/mL), which is significantly below the normal range and below the threshold typically used to define ACTH-independent Cushing syndrome (< 5 pg/mL).
5. An undetectable ACTH level indicates that the adrenal glands are autonomously producing excess cortisol, suppressing pituitary ACTH secretion via negative feedback.
6. Therefore, the source of the excess cortisol must be within the adrenal cortex itself (e.g., an adenoma or carcinoma).

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Pituitary adenoma secreting ACTH:** This is a cause of ACTH-dependent Cushing syndrome (Cushing disease). Patients with pituitary adenomas secrete excessive ACTH, leading to elevated or inappropriately normal plasma ACTH levels, not suppressed levels. This option is incorrect because the patient has undetectable ACTH.
- **(B) Ectopic source secreting ACTH (e.g., small cell lung cancer):** This is another cause of ACTH-dependent Cushing syndrome. Ectopic sources also secrete excessive ACTH, resulting in elevated or inappropriately normal plasma ACTH levels. This option is incorrect for the same reason as (A).
- **(C) Adrenal cortex adenoma or carcinoma:** This is a cause of ACTH-independent Cushing syndrome. Autonomous cortisol production by an adrenal tumor suppresses pituitary ACTH secretion, leading to undetectable plasma ACTH levels. This aligns perfectly with the patient's laboratory findings. This is the correct answer.
- **(D) Hypothalamus secreting corticotropin-releasing hormone (CRH):** While CRH stimulates ACTH release, primary hypothalamic CRH-secreting tumors are extremely rare causes of Cushing syndrome. Furthermore, such a condition would still lead to elevated ACTH and cortisol, not suppressed ACTH. This option is incorrect.
- **(E) Exogenous glucocorticoid administration:** This is the most common cause of Cushing syndrome overall. Exogenous steroids suppress the HPA axis, leading to low ACTH and adrenal atrophy. While the biochemical picture (low ACTH, high cortisol) is similar to ACTH-independent adrenal disease, the patient explicitly denies exogenous steroid use, making this diagnosis unlikely in this specific clinical context. Furthermore, the question asks for the anatomical *source* of the cortisol, which in this case would be the exogenous source, not the adrenal cortex. However, given the undetectable ACTH, the *pathophysiological mechanism* points towards adrenal independence, making (C) the better fit for the anatomical localization implied by the question stem after ruling out exogenous use.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Feature             | ACTH-Dependent Cushing (Pituitary/Ectopic) | ACTH-Independent Cushing (Adrenal) | Exogenous Glucocorticoids |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------|------------------------------------|---------------------------|
| Plasma ACTH         | Normal or Elevated (> 15-20 pg/mL)        | Suppressed (< 5 pg/mL, often < 1) | Suppressed (< 5 pg/mL)    |
| Adrenal Source      | Bilateral Hyperplasia                     | Unilateral Adenoma/Carcinoma, Bilateral Hyperplasia (rare) | Atrophy                   |
| HPA Axis Feedback   | Intact (but overwhelmed)                  | Suppressed                          | Suppressed                |
| Next Diagnostic Step| High-dose Dexamethasone Suppression, IPSS | Adrenal CT/MRI                     | History                   |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
The measurement of plasma ACTH in the setting of confirmed hypercortisolemia is crucial for differentiating ACTH-dependent (pituitary or ectopic source) from ACTH-independent (adrenal source) Cushing syndrome. Suppressed or undetectable ACTH levels (< 5 pg/mL) indicate autonomous adrenal cortisol production, while normal or elevated ACTH levels point towards an ACTH-secreting tumor. This distinction guides subsequent diagnostic imaging and testing.
