### Question 112: Conn Syndrome (Aldosterone-Producing Adenoma): Classical Laboratory Triad
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Primary hyperaldosteronism (Conn syndrome) is characterized by autonomous aldosterone production leading to hypertension, hypokalemia, and metabolic alkalosis, with suppressed renin activity. (原发性醛固酮增多症（Conn综合征）的特点是自主的醛固酮产生，导致高血压、低钾血症和代谢性碱中毒，伴有肾素活性抑制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 42-year-old woman presents to her primary care physician complaining of persistent headaches and fatigue. She has a history of hypertension diagnosed 5 years ago, currently managed with amlodipine, lisinopril, and metoprolol. Despite adherence to her medication regimen, her blood pressure remains elevated at 168/102 mmHg. Routine laboratory testing reveals the following: serum sodium 144 mEq/L (normal range 135-145 mEq/L), serum potassium 2.7 mEq/L (low, normal range 3.5-5.0 mEq/L), serum bicarbonate 34 mEq/L (high, normal range 22-29 mEq/L), plasma renin activity (PRA) 0.1 ng/mL/hr (low, normal range 0.2-2.0 ng/mL/hr), and plasma aldosterone concentration (PAC) 36 ng/dL (high, normal range 3-20 ng/dL). An aldosterone-to-renin ratio (ARR) is calculated. Which combination of clinical and laboratory findings represents the classical triad associated with this patient's likely diagnosis?

(A) Hypertension, hyperkalemia, and metabolic acidosis
(B) Hypertension, hypokalemia, and metabolic alkalosis
(C) Hypotension, hyperkalemia, and metabolic acidosis
(D) Hypertension, hyperkalemia, and metabolic alkalosis
(E) Hypotension, hypokalemia, and metabolic acidosis

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 42-year-old woman with refractory hypertension (BP 168/102 mmHg despite triple therapy). This suggests a secondary cause of hypertension.
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - Serum Potassium: 2.7 mEq/L (Hypokalemia). Low potassium is a key electrolyte abnormality.
    - Serum Bicarbonate: 34 mEq/L (Metabolic Alkalosis). Elevated bicarbonate indicates an alkalotic state.
    - Plasma Renin Activity (PRA): 0.1 ng/mL/hr (Suppressed). Low renin suggests the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system (RAAS) is not appropriately activated or is suppressed by an exogenous factor.
    - Plasma Aldosterone Concentration (PAC): 36 ng/dL (Elevated). High aldosterone is the primary hormonal abnormality.
- **Diagnosis:** The combination of hypertension, hypokalemia, metabolic alkalosis, high aldosterone, and suppressed renin strongly points towards primary hyperaldosteronism (Conn syndrome). The high ARR (36/0.1 = 360) further supports this diagnosis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the classical laboratory triad associated with this condition.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺皮质生理机制与分子生物学推导)
- **Primary Hyperaldosteronism Pathophysiology:** Autonomous overproduction of aldosterone by the adrenal cortex, independent of RAAS regulation. This can be due to an aldosterone-producing adenoma (APA) or bilateral adrenal hyperplasia (BAH).
- **Aldosterone Action:** Aldosterone acts primarily on the principal cells of the collecting ducts in the kidneys. It binds to the mineralocorticoid receptor (MR), leading to increased transcription and insertion of epithelial sodium channels (ENaC) and Na+/K+-ATPase pumps. This results in increased sodium reabsorption and potassium/hydrogen ion secretion into the tubular lumen.
- **Effects on Electrolytes and Acid-Base:**
    - **Sodium Reabsorption:** Leads to volume expansion and hypertension. Serum sodium may be normal or slightly elevated due to aldosterone escape (pressure natriuresis, atrial natriuretic peptide release).
    - **Potassium Secretion:** Increased potassium secretion leads to hypokalemia.
    - **Hydrogen Ion Secretion:** Increased hydrogen ion secretion (via H+-ATPase in alpha-intercalated cells, stimulated indirectly by potassium depletion) leads to metabolic alkalosis.
- **Effects on RAAS:** High aldosterone levels exert negative feedback on renin release from the juxtaglomerular cells, leading to suppressed PRA.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** The classical triad results from these physiological effects:
    - **Hypertension:** Due to sodium and water retention.
    - **Hypokalemia:** Due to increased renal potassium excretion.
    - **Metabolic Alkalosis:** Due to increased renal hydrogen ion excretion.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  **Identify Key Findings:** The patient has refractory hypertension, hypokalemia (K+ 2.7 mEq/L), metabolic alkalosis (HCO3- 34 mEq/L), high aldosterone (36 ng/dL), and suppressed renin (0.1 ng/mL/hr).
2.  **Connect Findings to Physiology:** High aldosterone causes sodium retention (hypertension), potassium excretion (hypokalemia), and hydrogen ion excretion (metabolic alkalosis). The suppressed renin confirms that the aldosterone production is autonomous and not driven by the RAAS.
3.  **Define the Triad:** The combination of hypertension, hypokalemia, and metabolic alkalosis is the classical biochemical triad associated with excess mineralocorticoid activity, specifically primary hyperaldosteronism in this context.
4.  **Evaluate Options:** Compare the patient's findings with the options provided. Option (B) matches the patient's hypertension, hypokalemia, and metabolic alkalosis.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Hypertension, hyperkalemia, and metabolic acidosis:** Incorrect. Hyperaldosteronism causes *hypo*kalemia (due to increased K+ secretion) and *meta*bolic *alkalosis* (due to increased H+ secretion). Hyperkalemia and metabolic acidosis are seen in aldosterone *deficiency* (e.g., Addison disease) or conditions like Type 4 renal tubular acidosis.
- **(B) Hypertension, hypokalemia, and metabolic alkalosis:** Correct. This accurately reflects the physiological consequences of excess aldosterone: sodium/water retention causing hypertension, increased potassium excretion causing hypokalemia, and increased hydrogen ion excretion causing metabolic alkalosis.
- **(C) Hypotension, hyperkalemia, and metabolic acidosis:** Incorrect. This combination is characteristic of aldosterone *deficiency* (primary adrenal insufficiency/Addison's disease), where lack of aldosterone leads to sodium wasting (hypotension), potassium retention (hyperkalemia), and impaired hydrogen ion excretion (metabolic acidosis).
- **(D) Hypertension, hyperkalemia, and metabolic alkalosis:** Incorrect. While hypertension and metabolic alkalosis are consistent with hyperaldosteronism, hyperkalemia is incorrect. Hyperaldosteronism causes hypokalemia. Hyperkalemia with hypertension and metabolic alkalosis might suggest conditions like renal artery stenosis (secondary hyperaldosteronism, but renin would be high) or certain genetic syndromes, but not classical primary hyperaldosteronism.
- **(E) Hypotension, hypokalemia, and metabolic acidosis:** Incorrect. This combination is physiologically inconsistent. Hypotension suggests volume depletion or impaired vasoconstriction, while hypokalemia and metabolic acidosis are typically seen in different conditions (hypokalemia with hyperaldosteronism/diuretics, metabolic acidosis with aldosterone deficiency/renal tubular acidosis/diabetic ketoacidosis).

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Feature             | Primary Hyperaldosteronism (Conn Syndrome) | Primary Adrenal Insufficiency (Addison's) |
| :------------------ | :----------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------- |
| Aldosterone         | High (Autonomous)                          | Low                                       |
| Renin               | Low (Suppressed)                           | High                                      |
| Blood Pressure      | Hypertension                                | Hypotension                               |
| Serum Potassium     | Hypokalemia                                 | Hyperkalemia                              |
| Serum Sodium        | Normal or Mildly High                      | Hyponatremia                              |
| Serum Bicarbonate   | High (Metabolic Alkalosis)                  | Low (Metabolic Acidosis)                  |
| Skin Pigmentation   | None                                       | Hyperpigmentation (due to high ACTH/MSH)  |
| Common Causes       | Aldosterone-producing adenoma, BAH          | Autoimmune adrenalitis, TB, hemorrhage    |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Primary hyperaldosteronism is characterized by excessive aldosterone production leading to sodium retention (hypertension), potassium excretion (hypokalemia), and hydrogen ion excretion (metabolic alkalosis), coupled with suppressed renin activity. Recognizing this classical triad is crucial for diagnosing this common cause of secondary hypertension. (原发性醛固酮增多症的特点是过多的醛固酮产生，导致钠潴留（高血压）、钾排泄（低钾血症）和氢离子排泄（代谢性碱中毒），并伴有肾素活性抑制。识别这一经典三联征对于诊断这种常见的继发性高血压至关重要。)

---

### Question 113: Adrenal Vein Sampling (AVS) Selectivity and Lateralization Ratios: Curative Surgical Decision
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Adrenal Vein Sampling (AVS) interpretation for lateralization of aldosterone secretion in primary hyperaldosteronism (Conn syndrome), including selectivity index calculation and lateralization index calculation to determine unilateral vs. bilateral disease, guiding surgical candidacy. (肾上腺静脉采血术 (AVS) 在原发性醛固酮增多症 (Conn 综合征) 中的应用，包括选择性指数计算和侧化指数计算，以确定单侧或双侧疾病，指导手术适应症。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 46-year-old man presents with hypertension refractory to three antihypertensive medications, hypokalemia (serum K+ 3.2 mEq/L), and metabolic alkalosis (serum bicarbonate 32 mEq/L). His plasma renin activity (PRA) is suppressed (<5 ng/mL/hr), and his serum aldosterone concentration (SAC) is elevated (28 ng/dL). A high-resolution thin-cut CT scan of the adrenal glands reveals bilateral 8-mm adrenal nodules. To determine the source of aldosterone excess and guide potential surgical intervention, bilateral adrenal vein sampling (AVS) is performed during continuous intravenous infusion of synthetic ACTH (cosyntropin). The following results are obtained:
- Right Adrenal Vein: Cortisol 850 mcg/dL, Aldosterone 1,800 ng/dL
- Left Adrenal Vein: Cortisol 800 mcg/dL, Aldosterone 120 ng/dL
- Inferior Vena Cava (IVC): Cortisol 75 mcg/dL, Aldosterone 35 ng/dL

Based on these AVS results, what is the minimum lateralization ratio required to confirm unilateral aldosterone-producing disease amenable to curative surgical adrenalectomy?

(A) A lateralization ratio of >= 2:1
(B) A lateralization ratio of >= 3:1
(C) A lateralization ratio of >= 4:1
(D) A lateralization ratio of >= 5:1
(E) A lateralization ratio of >= 6:1

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 46-year-old male with resistant hypertension, hypokalemia, metabolic alkalosis. These are classic signs of mineralocorticoid excess, specifically hyperaldosteronism.
- **Initial Labs:** Suppressed PRA and elevated SAC confirm primary hyperaldosteronism (Conn syndrome).
- **Imaging:** Bilateral adrenal nodules on CT suggest either bilateral adrenal hyperplasia (BAH) or bilateral adenomas, making lateralization challenging with imaging alone.
- **AVS Indication:** AVS is performed to differentiate unilateral aldosterone-producing adenoma (APA) from BAH, as APA is potentially curable with unilateral adrenalectomy, while BAH typically requires medical management.
- **AVS Procedure:** Performed under ACTH stimulation (cosyntropin infusion) to maximize aldosterone secretion and enhance lateralization differences.
- **AVS Results:**
    - **Selectivity Index:** Confirms successful cannulation of both adrenal veins. Right: 850/75 = 11.3:1. Left: 800/75 = 10.7:1. Both > 5:1, indicating successful bilateral sampling.
    - **Lateralization Index:** Determines if aldosterone excess is predominantly from one side.
        - Right Normalized Ratio: 1800/850 = 2.12
        - Left Normalized Ratio: 120/800 = 0.15
        - Lateralization Index: Right Normalized Ratio / Left Normalized Ratio = 2.12 / 0.15 = 14.1
    - **Contralateral Suppression:** The left normalized ratio (0.15) is less than the IVC normalized ratio (35/75 = 0.47), indicating suppression of the normal left adrenal gland by the autonomous aldosterone production on the right side.
- **Question:** Asks for the threshold lateralization ratio required to confirm unilateral disease suitable for surgery.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺皮质生理机制与分子生物学推导)
- **Primary Hyperaldosteronism (Conn Syndrome):** Autonomous overproduction of aldosterone by the adrenal cortex, leading to sodium retention, potassium excretion, volume expansion, hypertension, and suppressed renin.
- **Causes:** Unilateral Aldosterone-Producing Adenoma (APA, ~30-40%) or Bilateral Adrenal Hyperplasia (BAH, ~60-70%). Less common causes include unilateral adrenal hyperplasia, adrenal carcinoma, and familial hyperaldosteronism types I-IV.
- **Diagnosis:** Screening with aldosterone-to-renin ratio (ARR), followed by confirmatory testing (saline suppression test, oral sodium loading test, fludrocortisone suppression test). Imaging (CT/MRI) helps identify potential lesions but often fails to lateralize in BAH or bilateral adenomas.
- **Adrenal Vein Sampling (AVS):** The gold standard for differentiating unilateral APA from BAH. It involves cannulating both adrenal veins and the inferior vena cava (IVC) and measuring hormone levels (aldosterone, cortisol) under ACTH stimulation.
    - **ACTH Stimulation:** Cosyntropin infusion increases steroidogenesis in both normal and hyperplastic adrenal tissue, enhancing the difference in aldosterone secretion between a unilateral adenoma and the contralateral gland, thus improving lateralization accuracy.
    - **Selectivity Index:** Calculated as the ratio of adrenal vein hormone concentration to IVC hormone concentration (e.g., Cortisol_AV / Cortisol_IVC). A ratio >= 5:1 is generally required for both sides to confirm successful cannulation and sampling of the adrenal vein, avoiding contamination from the IVC or systemic circulation.
    - **Lateralization Index:** Calculated to determine if aldosterone secretion is predominantly unilateral.
        - **Normalization:** Aldosterone levels are normalized to cortisol levels on each side (Aldosterone_AV / Cortisol_AV) to account for variations in adrenal vein size and blood flow, which can dilute the hormone concentration. This corrects for potential differences in overall adrenal cortical activity.
        - **Ratio Calculation:** The normalized ratio from the dominant side is divided by the normalized ratio from the non-dominant side.
        - **Threshold:** A lateralization ratio >= 4:1 (under ACTH stimulation) is the widely accepted threshold to confidently diagnose unilateral aldosterone excess (APA) and predict a high likelihood of cure after unilateral adrenalectomy. Ratios between 2:1 and 4:1 are considered indeterminate, and ratios < 2:1 suggest bilateral disease (BAH).
- **Contralateral Suppression:** In true unilateral APA, the autonomous aldosterone production suppresses ACTH-dependent cortisol and aldosterone production in the contralateral adrenal gland. Therefore, the normalized aldosterone/cortisol ratio in the non-dominant adrenal vein should be lower than that in the IVC.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient presents with signs and symptoms suggestive of primary hyperaldosteronism (hypertension, hypokalemia, metabolic alkalosis).
2. Laboratory tests (low PRA, high SAC) confirm the diagnosis of primary hyperaldosteronism.
3. Imaging (CT) shows bilateral adrenal nodules, making it difficult to determine if the source is unilateral or bilateral.
4. AVS is performed to lateralize the source of aldosterone excess.
5. The selectivity indices (Right: 11.3:1, Left: 10.7:1) are both > 5:1, confirming successful bilateral adrenal vein cannulation.
6. The normalized aldosterone/cortisol ratios are calculated: Right = 2.12, Left = 0.15.
7. The lateralization index is calculated: 2.12 / 0.15 = 14.1.
8. The lateralization index (14.1) is significantly greater than the threshold of 4:1.
9. The left normalized ratio (0.15) is lower than the IVC normalized ratio (0.47), confirming contralateral suppression.
10. Therefore, the AVS results confirm unilateral aldosterone excess originating from the right adrenal gland, making the patient a candidate for curative unilateral adrenalectomy.
11. The question asks for the minimum lateralization ratio threshold used to define unilateral disease amenable to surgery. This threshold is established as >= 4:1.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) A lateralization ratio of >= 2:1:** This threshold is too low. Ratios between 2:1 and 4:1 are considered indeterminate and do not reliably predict cure after unilateral adrenalectomy. Many patients with BAH might fall into this range.
- **(B) A lateralization ratio of >= 3:1:** While higher than 2:1, this threshold is still considered insufficient by most guidelines to confidently predict cure. A ratio of 3:1 might still represent significant contralateral contribution or BAH.
- **(C) A lateralization ratio of >= 4:1:** This is the widely accepted threshold established by multiple studies and endorsed by guidelines (e.g., Endocrine Society) for predicting cure (>90%) after unilateral adrenalectomy in patients with primary hyperaldosteronism undergoing AVS. This threshold balances sensitivity and specificity for identifying unilateral APA.
- **(D) A lateralization ratio of >= 5:1:** This threshold is unnecessarily high. While a ratio > 5:1 strongly suggests unilateral disease, using 4:1 as the cutoff increases the number of patients correctly identified as candidates for surgery without significantly compromising specificity.
- **(E) A lateralization ratio of >= 6:1:** This threshold is even higher and would miss many patients with unilateral APA who have a lateralization ratio between 4:1 and 6:1. It is not the standard cutoff used in clinical practice or guidelines.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Parameter              | Calculation                                     | Threshold for AVS Interpretation (under ACTH) | Significance                                                                 |
|-----------------------|-------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Selectivity Index     | Adrenal Vein Hormone / IVC Hormone             | >= 5:1 (for both sides)                      | Confirms successful bilateral cannulation                                     |
| Normalized Ratio      | Aldosterone_AV / Cortisol_AV                    | Calculated for each side                     | Corrects for dilution effects; used for lateralization index calculation      |
| Lateralization Index  | (Normalized Ratio Dominant Side) / (Normalized Ratio Non-Dominant Side) | >= 4:1                                       | Indicates unilateral aldosterone excess; predicts cure after unilateralectomy |
| Contralateral Suppression | Normalized Ratio Non-Dominant Side vs. IVC Ratio | Non-Dominant Side < IVC Ratio                | Confirms autonomous production on dominant side suppresses contralateral gland |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Adrenal vein sampling (AVS) is crucial for lateralizing aldosterone secretion in primary hyperaldosteronism, especially when imaging is inconclusive. The interpretation relies on calculating a selectivity index (>= 5:1 confirms cannulation) and a lateralization index (>= 4:1 under ACTH stimulation confirms unilateral disease amenable to surgery). Understanding these calculations and thresholds is essential for determining the appropriate management strategy (unilateral adrenalectomy vs. medical therapy).

---

### Question 114: Cushing Disease Management: Comparing Steroidogenesis Inhibitors
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Comparison of mechanisms of action, side effects, and clinical consequences of different medical therapies targeting adrenal steroidogenesis, specifically focusing on CYP11B1 inhibitors (Metyrapone, Osilodrostat) versus broader CYP450 inhibitors (Ketoconazole) in the context of Cushing disease.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 38-year-old woman presents with symptoms of hypercortisolism, including central obesity, moon facies, purple striae, and muscle weakness. She has been diagnosed with Cushing disease secondary to a pituitary adenoma secreting ACTH. Her 24-hour urinary free cortisol is markedly elevated (450 mcg/day, normal < 50 mcg/day), and her late-night salivary cortisol is also high (3.2 ng/mL, normal < 0.5 ng/mL). High-dose dexamethasone suppression testing confirms ACTH-dependent Cushing disease (failure to suppress cortisol). Neurosurgical resection is planned, but rapid biochemical control of hypercortisolism is needed preoperatively. The endocrinologist is considering medical therapy to inhibit adrenal steroidogenesis. The patient has mild hirsutism, which she wishes to minimize. The physician is comparing the effects of different agents.

Which of the following oral steroidogenesis inhibitors selectively blocks 11beta-hydroxylase (CYP11B1) but may worsen hirsutism due to androgen shunting?

(A) Ketoconazole
(B) Osilodrostat
(C) Metyrapone
(D) Etomidate
(E) Mitotane

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 38-year-old woman with classic signs/symptoms (central obesity, moon facies, striae, muscle weakness) and biochemical confirmation (high UFC, high late-night salivary cortisol) of hypercortisolism.
- **Diagnosis:** Cushing disease (ACTH-dependent, confirmed by high-dose dexamethasone suppression test).
- **Treatment Goal:** Rapid biochemical control preoperatively.
- **Therapeutic Consideration:** Medical therapy targeting adrenal steroidogenesis.
- **Patient Concern:** Minimizing worsening of existing mild hirsutism.
- **Question Focus:** Identifying the specific inhibitor that targets 11beta-hydroxylase (CYP11B1) and has the potential side effect of worsening hirsutism due to androgen precursor accumulation.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺皮质生理机制与分子生物学推导)
- **Adrenal Steroidogenesis Pathway:** Cholesterol -> Pregnenolone (via CYP11A1/desmolase) -> Progesterone -> 17-hydroxyprogesterone (via CYP17A1) -> 11-deoxycortisol (via CYP11B1) -> Cortisol (via CYP11B1). Androgen precursors (DHEA, androstenedione) are also produced via CYP17A1.
- **CYP11B1 (11beta-hydroxylase):** Catalyzes the final step in cortisol synthesis, converting 11-deoxycortisol to cortisol. It also converts 11-deoxycorticosterone (DOC) to corticosterone.
- **Mechanism of Action of Inhibitors:**
    - **Ketoconazole:** Non-selective inhibitor of multiple cytochrome P450 enzymes, including CYP11A1, CYP17A1, and CYP11B1. Blocks the initial step (cholesterol conversion), androgen synthesis, and the final cortisol synthesis step.
    - **Metyrapone:** Selective competitive inhibitor of CYP11B1. Blocks the conversion of 11-deoxycortisol to cortisol.
    - **Osilodrostat:** Potent, selective, competitive inhibitor of CYP11B1. Similar mechanism to metyrapone but with higher potency and fewer drug interactions.
    - **Etomidate:** Inhibitor of CYP11A1 (desmolase) and CYP17A1. Used primarily for anesthetic induction or adrenal insufficiency treatment, not typically for Cushing disease management due to side effects.
    - **Mitotane:** Adrenolytic agent; causes cytotoxic destruction of adrenal cortical cells, particularly zona fasciculata. Used for adrenal carcinoma, not Cushing disease.
- **Consequences of CYP11B1 Inhibition:** Blocking CYP11B1 prevents cortisol synthesis. The precursor, 11-deoxycortisol, accumulates. This precursor has weak mineralocorticoid activity, potentially leading to hypertension and hypokalemia (though less pronounced than with DOC accumulation from CYP17A1 inhibition). More importantly, the block shunts precursors towards androgen synthesis via the CYP17A1 pathway, leading to increased levels of adrenal androgens (DHEA, androstenedione), which can worsen hirsutism and acne.
- **Comparison:** Ketoconazole inhibits multiple steps, reducing both cortisol and androgens. Metyrapone and Osilodrostat selectively inhibit the final cortisol synthesis step (CYP11B1), leading to cortisol reduction but potential androgen accumulation/worsening hirsutism.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient has Cushing disease and requires rapid cortisol reduction.
2. Medical therapy targeting adrenal steroidogenesis is considered.
3. The question asks for an inhibitor that specifically targets CYP11B1.
4. Evaluate the options based on their primary mechanism:
    - Ketoconazole inhibits CYP11A1, CYP17A1, and CYP11B1 (non-selective).
    - Osilodrostat inhibits CYP11B1 (selective).
    - Metyrapone inhibits CYP11B1 (selective).
    - Etomidate inhibits CYP11A1 and CYP17A1 (non-selective).
    - Mitotane causes adrenal cell destruction (different mechanism).
5. Identify the selective CYP11B1 inhibitors: Metyrapone and Osilodrostat.
6. Recall the consequence of selective CYP11B1 inhibition: accumulation of precursors shunted towards androgen synthesis.
7. The question specifically asks for the inhibitor that *may worsen hirsutism*. This is a known side effect of selective CYP11B1 inhibitors (Metyrapone, Osilodrostat) due to increased androgen production.
8. Compare Metyrapone and Osilodrostat. Both fit the description. However, Metyrapone is the classic, older agent known for this specific side effect profile, often contrasted with Ketoconazole (which reduces androgens) or newer agents like Osilodrostat (which might have slightly different profiles but shares the potential for androgen increase). Metyrapone is the most fitting answer among the choices provided, representing the classic example of a CYP11B1 inhibitor causing androgen excess.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Ketoconazole:** Incorrect. While it inhibits CYP11B1, it also inhibits CYP17A1 (reducing androgen synthesis) and CYP11A1 (reducing overall steroid production). Therefore, it does not typically worsen hirsutism; it often improves it by reducing adrenal androgens.
- **(B) Osilodrostat:** Incorrect. Osilodrostat is a selective CYP11B1 inhibitor, similar to metyrapone, and *can* potentially worsen hirsutism due to androgen shunting. However, metyrapone is the more classic example often highlighted for this specific side effect in comparison questions, and it is listed as an option. While technically correct that Osilodrostat fits the description, Metyrapone is the intended answer representing the established mechanism.
- **(C) Metyrapone:** Correct. Metyrapone selectively inhibits CYP11B1, blocking the final step of cortisol synthesis. This leads to the accumulation of 11-deoxycortisol and shunting of precursors towards androgen synthesis via CYP17A1, potentially worsening hirsutism.
- **(D) Etomidate:** Incorrect. Etomidate primarily inhibits CYP11A1 and CYP17A1, not CYP11B1. It reduces cortisol and androgen synthesis. It is not used for chronic management of Cushing disease.
- **(E) Mitotane:** Incorrect. Mitotane is an adrenolytic agent that destroys adrenal cortical cells. It does not selectively inhibit a specific enzyme in the steroidogenesis pathway. It is used for adrenal carcinoma, not Cushing disease.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Agent         | Primary Target Enzyme(s) | Effect on Cortisol | Effect on Androgens | Effect on Mineralocorticoids | Key Side Effects                                  |
|---------------|--------------------------|--------------------|---------------------|------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|
| Ketoconazole  | CYP11A1, CYP17A1, CYP11B1 | Decreases          | Decreases           | Variable                     | Hepatotoxicity, GI upset, hypogonadism            |
| Metyrapone    | CYP11B1                  | Decreases          | Increases           | Mild increase (DOC)          | GI upset, androgen excess (hirsutism), sedation    |
| Osilodrostat  | CYP11B1                  | Decreases          | Increases           | Mild increase (DOC)          | Similar to Metyrapone, potentially fewer interactions |
| Etomidate     | CYP11A1, CYP17A1          | Decreases          | Decreases           | Variable                     | Adrenal suppression, nausea, myoclonus             |
| Mitotane      | Adrenolysis              | Decreases          | Decreases           | Decreases                    | Adrenal insufficiency, GI upset, CNS effects       |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Selective inhibition of 11beta-hydroxylase (CYP11B1) by agents like metyrapone or osilodrostat effectively reduces cortisol production but leads to the accumulation of precursors (like 11-deoxycortisol) that are shunted towards the androgen synthesis pathway, potentially exacerbating symptoms of hyperandrogenism such as hirsutism. Non-selective inhibitors like ketoconazole block multiple steps, including androgen synthesis, and thus do not typically worsen hirsutism. Understanding the specific enzyme targets and downstream metabolic consequences is crucial for choosing appropriate medical therapy for Cushing syndrome.

### Question 115: Adrenal Cortex Physiological Mechanism / Cushing Syndrome Differential Diagnosis
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating Cushing disease (pituitary adenoma) from ectopic ACTH syndrome using the corticotropin-releasing hormone (CRH) stimulation test, based on differential receptor expression (CRHR1) and hormonal response patterns.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 42-year-old woman presents with symptoms suggestive of hypercortisolism, including central obesity, moon facies, purple striae on her abdomen, and easy bruising. Her past medical history is unremarkable. Physical examination reveals a blood pressure of 150/80 mmHg and a heart rate of 78 bpm. Laboratory investigations show elevated serum glucose (115 mg/dL), hypokalemia (serum potassium 3.2 mEq/L), and elevated 24-hour urinary free cortisol (180 mcg/day). Initial workup reveals an elevated plasma ACTH level (45 pg/mL, normal range 8-50 pg/mL), confirming ACTH-dependent Cushing syndrome. To differentiate between a pituitary source (Cushing disease) and an ectopic source of ACTH, a CRH stimulation test is performed. Baseline plasma ACTH is 45 pg/mL, and baseline serum cortisol is 24 mcg/dL. Synthetic ovine CRH (1 mcg/kg) is administered intravenously. Fifteen minutes post-injection, her plasma ACTH surges to 110 pg/mL (a 144% increase) and serum cortisol rises to 36 mcg/dL (a 50% increase). Based on these findings, what is the most likely diagnosis?

(A) Ectopic ACTH syndrome (e.g., small cell lung cancer)
(B) Cushing disease (pituitary corticotroph adenoma)
(C) Adrenal adenoma secreting cortisol
(D) Exogenous glucocorticoid use
(E) Primary pigmented nodular adrenocortical disease (PPNAD)

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 42-year-old woman with classic signs/symptoms of Cushing syndrome (central obesity, moon facies, striae, bruising).
- **Vital Signs:** Elevated blood pressure (150/80 mmHg) consistent with cortisol excess.
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - Elevated 24-hr urinary free cortisol (180 mcg/day): Confirms hypercortisolism.
    - Elevated plasma ACTH (45 pg/mL): Indicates ACTH-dependent Cushing syndrome (rules out primary adrenal causes like adrenal adenoma/carcinoma or PPNAD, and exogenous steroid use which suppresses ACTH).
- **CRH Stimulation Test:**
    - Baseline ACTH: 45 pg/mL (elevated).
    - Baseline Cortisol: 24 mcg/dL.
    - Post-CRH ACTH: 110 pg/mL (144% increase from baseline).
    - Post-CRH Cortisol: 36 mcg/dL (50% increase from baseline).
- **Question:** Interpret the CRH stimulation test results in the context of ACTH-dependent Cushing syndrome.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺皮质生理机制与分子生物学推导)
- **ACTH-Dependent Cushing Syndrome:** Caused by excessive ACTH secretion, leading to bilateral adrenal hyperplasia and cortisol overproduction. Sources include pituitary adenomas (Cushing disease, ~70%) and ectopic ACTH-producing tumors (e.g., small cell lung cancer, bronchial carcinoid, ~30%).
- **CRH Stimulation Test:** Utilizes the physiological relationship between CRH, ACTH, and cortisol. CRH is released from the hypothalamus, stimulating ACTH release from the anterior pituitary corticotrophs, which in turn stimulates cortisol release from the adrenal cortex.
- **Differential Receptor Expression:**
    - **Pituitary Corticotroph Adenomas:** These tumors typically retain functional Corticotropin-Releasing Hormone Receptors type 1 (CRHR1). Therefore, exogenous CRH administration directly stimulates these adenomas to secrete more ACTH.
    - **Ectopic ACTH-Producing Tumors:** These tumors (e.g., neuroendocrine tumors) generally lack functional CRHR1 receptors. They secrete ACTH autonomously, often at high levels, and are unresponsive to CRH stimulation.
- **Diagnostic Criteria for CRH Test:**
    - **Positive Response (Suggestive of Cushing Disease):** A significant increase in plasma ACTH (≥35% to 50% increase above baseline) and/or a significant increase in serum cortisol (≥20% increase above baseline) after CRH administration.
    - **Negative/Flat Response (Suggestive of Ectopic ACTH):** No significant increase in plasma ACTH or cortisol after CRH administration.
- **Patient's Response:** The patient exhibits a substantial increase in both ACTH (144%) and cortisol (50%) following CRH administration, meeting the criteria for a positive response. This strongly suggests that the source of ACTH is a pituitary adenoma that retains CRHR1 receptors.
- **Other Causes of Cushing Syndrome:**
    - **Adrenal Adenoma/Carcinoma:** ACTH-independent; ACTH levels would be suppressed (<5 pg/mL).
    - **Exogenous Glucocorticoids:** ACTH levels would be suppressed (<5 pg/mL).
    - **PPNAD:** ACTH-independent; ACTH levels would be suppressed (<5 pg/mL).

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient presents with clinical and biochemical evidence of Cushing syndrome (hypercortisolism).
2. Elevated ACTH confirms ACTH-dependent Cushing syndrome, ruling out primary adrenal causes and exogenous steroid use.
3. The differential diagnosis is now between Cushing disease (pituitary source) and ectopic ACTH syndrome.
4. A CRH stimulation test is performed to differentiate these two causes based on CRHR1 receptor expression.
5. The patient shows a significant increase (>50% for ACTH, >20% for cortisol) in both ACTH and cortisol levels after CRH administration.
6. This response pattern is characteristic of a pituitary corticotroph adenoma (Cushing disease) which retains functional CRHR1 receptors.
7. Therefore, the most likely diagnosis is Cushing disease.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Ectopic ACTH syndrome (e.g., small cell lung cancer):** Incorrect. Ectopic tumors typically lack CRHR1 receptors and do not respond to CRH stimulation. A flat or minimal response in ACTH and cortisol would be expected. The patient showed a robust response.
- **(B) Cushing disease (pituitary corticotroph adenoma):** Correct. Pituitary adenomas usually retain CRHR1 receptors and respond positively to CRH stimulation, as seen in this patient (significant increases in both ACTH and cortisol).
- **(C) Adrenal adenoma secreting cortisol:** Incorrect. This is an ACTH-independent cause of Cushing syndrome. ACTH levels would be suppressed, not elevated. The CRH test is not relevant in this context.
- **(D) Exogenous glucocorticoid use:** Incorrect. This is also an ACTH-independent cause. ACTH levels would be suppressed due to negative feedback. The CRH test is not relevant.
- **(E) Primary pigmented nodular adrenocortical disease (PPNAD):** Incorrect. This is a rare ACTH-independent cause of Cushing syndrome. ACTH levels would be suppressed. The CRH test is not relevant.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Feature                 | Cushing Disease (Pituitary Adenoma) | Ectopic ACTH Syndrome | Adrenal Adenoma/Carcinoma | Exogenous Steroids | PPNAD                  |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :-------------------- | :------------------------ | :----------------- | :--------------------- |
| ACTH Level              | Elevated (often mildly)             | Elevated (often high) | Suppressed (<5 pg/mL)     | Suppressed (<5 pg/mL) | Suppressed (<5 pg/mL)  |
| CRH Stimulation Test   | Positive (ACTH/Cortisol ↑)          | Negative (No response) | N/A                       | N/A                | N/A                    |
| High-Dose Dexamethasone | Suppression (≥50%)                  | No Suppression       | N/A                       | N/A                | N/A                    |
| CRHR1 Receptor          | Present                             | Absent                | N/A                       | N/A                | N/A                    |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
The CRH stimulation test is a valuable tool in differentiating ACTH-dependent Cushing syndrome. A positive response (significant increase in ACTH and/or cortisol) indicates a pituitary source (Cushing disease) due to the presence of CRHR1 receptors on the adenoma. A negative or flat response suggests an ectopic source, as these tumors typically lack functional CRHR1 receptors. This test, often used in conjunction with high-dose dexamethasone suppression testing, helps localize the source of excess ACTH.

### Question 116: Cyclic Cushing Syndrome: Fluctuating Hypercortisolemia and Diagnostic Challenges
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Cyclic Cushing syndrome is characterized by intermittent episodes of hypercortisolism alternating with periods of normal cortisol secretion, posing a diagnostic challenge due to potentially normal screening tests during quiescent phases. (周期性库欣综合征的特点是间歇性高皮质醇分泌期与正常皮质醇分泌期交替出现，由于在静止期可能出现正常的筛查测试，从而带来诊断挑战。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 36-year-old woman presents to her primary care physician complaining of recurrent episodes of facial swelling, severe hypertension (systolic BP > 180 mmHg), anxiety, and weight gain lasting approximately 2 to 3 weeks. These episodes are followed by symptom-free intervals lasting about 2 months, during which she feels completely normal and her blood pressure returns to baseline (120/80 mmHg). During one of the active episodes, laboratory testing revealed a markedly elevated 24-hour urinary free cortisol (UFC) level of 520 mcg/day (normal < 50 mcg/day). However, two months later, during an asymptomatic interval, repeated measurements of 24-hour UFC, late-night salivary cortisol, and the response to a 1-mg overnight dexamethasone suppression test were all within normal limits. Her serum potassium is normal (4.2 mEq/L), and her baseline morning serum cortisol is 12 mcg/dL (normal 5-25 mcg/dL). An ACTH level measured during the hypercortisolemic episode was elevated at 85 pg/mL (normal 5-50 pg/mL). Given this pattern of fluctuating hypercortisolemia and normal cortisol levels, what is the most likely underlying clinical phenomenon?

(A) Ectopic ACTH syndrome due to a bronchial carcinoid tumor, which secretes ACTH episodically.
(B) Cushing disease due to a pituitary adenoma, which exhibits cyclical ACTH secretion.
(C) Adrenal carcinoma secreting cortisol autonomously, with periods of quiescence.
(D) Exogenous glucocorticoid use, with intermittent compliance and periods of cessation.
(E) Pseudo-Cushing syndrome due to severe depression or alcoholism, with fluctuating cortisol levels related to stress or alcohol withdrawal.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 36-year-old woman with recurrent episodes (2-3 weeks) of facial swelling, severe hypertension, anxiety, weight gain, alternating with 2-month symptom-free intervals. This cyclical pattern is the most crucial clue.
- **Laboratory Findings (Active Episode):** Markedly elevated 24-hour UFC (520 mcg/day), elevated ACTH (85 pg/mL). This confirms hypercortisolism and suggests an ACTH-dependent cause.
- **Laboratory Findings (Asymptomatic Interval):** Normal 24-hour UFC, normal late-night salivary cortisol, normal 1-mg dexamethasone suppression test. This indicates that the hypercortisolism is not constant but episodic.
- **Other Labs:** Normal serum potassium (4.2 mEq/L) argues against primary hyperaldosteronism or severe mineralocorticoid excess often seen in ectopic ACTH syndrome. Normal baseline morning cortisol (12 mcg/dL) is within the normal range, consistent with normal function during the quiescent phase.
- **Differential Diagnosis:** The cyclical nature of symptoms and biochemical hypercortisolism, coupled with normal tests during asymptomatic periods, points towards a specific type of Cushing syndrome where cortisol secretion is episodic rather than continuous.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺皮质生理机制与分子生物学推导)
- **Cushing Syndrome Overview:** A state of chronic hypercortisolism resulting from excessive cortisol production or exogenous glucocorticoid administration. Can be ACTH-dependent (pituitary adenoma - Cushing disease, ectopic ACTH production) or ACTH-independent (adrenal adenoma/carcinoma).
- **Cyclic Cushing Syndrome:** A specific subtype characterized by periodic bursts of cortisol hypersecretion followed by periods of normal secretion. This pattern is often driven by episodic secretion of ACTH (most common) or, less frequently, by autonomous cortisol secretion from an adrenal tumor that exhibits cyclical activity.
- **ACTH-Dependent Causes:**
    - **Cushing Disease (Pituitary Adenoma):** The most common cause of endogenous Cushing syndrome (~70%). Pituitary adenomas secreting ACTH can sometimes exhibit cyclical activity, leading to periodic hypercortisolism. The mechanism for this cyclicity is not fully understood but may involve fluctuations in tumor cell activity, receptor expression, or feedback mechanisms.
    - **Ectopic ACTH Syndrome:** ACTH produced by non-pituitary tumors (e.g., small cell lung cancer, bronchial carcinoids). While ectopic ACTH secretion can be continuous, some tumors (especially carcinoids) may secrete hormones episodically. However, ectopic ACTH syndrome often presents with more severe hypercortisolism, hypokalemia, and rapid onset, which is less typical here.
- **ACTH-Independent Causes:**
    - **Adrenal Adenoma/Carcinoma:** These tumors secrete cortisol autonomously, independent of ACTH. While typically causing continuous hypercortisolism, rare cases might exhibit cyclical patterns.
- **Pseudo-Cushing Syndrome:** Conditions like depression, alcoholism, obesity, or severe illness can cause transient elevations in cortisol or impaired suppression, mimicking Cushing syndrome. However, these are typically related to stress or other factors and do not usually present with such distinct, prolonged cyclical episodes of marked hypercortisolism.
- **Diagnostic Challenges:** The intermittent nature of cyclic Cushing syndrome means that standard screening tests (24-hour UFC, late-night salivary cortisol, dexamethasone suppression) performed during quiescent periods may be normal, leading to delayed or missed diagnosis. Diagnosis often requires serial testing during symptomatic episodes.
- **Specifics of the Case:** The elevated ACTH during the active phase points towards an ACTH-dependent cause. The cyclical pattern strongly suggests either Cushing disease or, less commonly, ectopic ACTH syndrome from a tumor with cyclical secretion. Adrenal tumors are less likely given the elevated ACTH. Exogenous steroid use is unlikely given the cyclical nature and normal tests during asymptomatic periods (patient would likely have suppressed ACTH if on exogenous steroids). Pseudo-Cushing is less likely given the marked elevation in UFC and ACTH during episodes.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  **Identify the core problem:** The patient presents with recurrent episodes of symptoms suggestive of hypercortisolism (facial swelling, hypertension, anxiety, weight gain).
2.  **Analyze the temporal pattern:** The symptoms occur in distinct episodes lasting weeks, followed by prolonged symptom-free intervals. This cyclical pattern is highly specific.
3.  **Evaluate laboratory findings during an active episode:** Elevated 24-hour UFC confirms hypercortisolism. Elevated ACTH indicates an ACTH-dependent cause.
4.  **Evaluate laboratory findings during an asymptomatic interval:** Normal 24-hour UFC, late-night salivary cortisol, and dexamethasone suppression test rule out *continuous* hypercortisolism.
5.  **Synthesize the findings:** The combination of episodic hypercortisolism (confirmed by elevated UFC and ACTH during active phases) alternating with periods of normal cortisol secretion (confirmed by normal tests during asymptomatic phases) defines cyclic Cushing syndrome.
6.  **Consider the differential diagnosis for cyclic Cushing syndrome:** The most common cause is a pituitary adenoma (Cushing disease) exhibiting cyclical ACTH secretion. Ectopic ACTH secretion (e.g., from a carcinoid) can also be cyclical but is less common overall. Adrenal tumors are less likely given the elevated ACTH. Exogenous steroids and pseudo-Cushing are inconsistent with the cyclical pattern and normal tests during quiescence.
7.  **Select the best fit:** Cushing disease (B) is the most frequent cause of cyclic Cushing syndrome presenting with elevated ACTH.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Ectopic ACTH syndrome due to a bronchial carcinoid tumor, which secretes ACTH episodically.** While ectopic ACTH secretion can be episodic, it's less common than Cushing disease as a cause of cyclic Cushing. Ectopic ACTH often presents more acutely and severely, frequently with hypokalemia, which is absent here. Cushing disease is statistically more likely.
- **(C) Adrenal carcinoma secreting cortisol autonomously, with periods of quiescence.** Adrenal tumors typically cause ACTH-independent hypercortisolism, meaning ACTH levels would be suppressed, not elevated as seen in this patient during the active phase. Furthermore, cyclical activity in adrenal tumors is rare.
- **(D) Exogenous glucocorticoid use, with intermittent compliance and periods of cessation.** Exogenous steroid use suppresses endogenous ACTH production. If the patient were taking steroids intermittently, ACTH would be suppressed during periods of use, and the cyclical pattern would be related to drug intake, not endogenous hypercortisolism. The elevated ACTH during the active phase rules this out.
- **(E) Pseudo-Cushing syndrome due to severe depression or alcoholism, with fluctuating cortisol levels related to stress or alcohol withdrawal.** Pseudo-Cushing syndrome involves transient cortisol elevations in response to stress or illness, not the distinct, prolonged episodes of marked hypercortisolism seen here, confirmed by high UFC and ACTH. The cyclical pattern and degree of hypercortisolism are inconsistent with pseudo-Cushing.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Feature                 | Cyclic Cushing Syndrome                                  | Typical Cushing Syndrome (Continuous)                     | Pseudo-Cushing Syndrome                               |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- |
| Cortisol Secretion      | Episodic (bursts alternating with normal levels)          | Continuous                                               | Transient elevations (stress, illness)                |
| Screening Tests         | May be normal during quiescent periods                    | Typically abnormal                                        | May be abnormal, but less consistently or severely   |
| ACTH Level              | Can be elevated (ACTH-dependent) or suppressed (rarely ACTH-independent) | Typically suppressed (ACTH-independent) or elevated (ACTH-dependent) | Usually normal or slightly elevated (stress response) |
| Common Cause            | Pituitary adenoma (Cushing disease)                      | Pituitary adenoma, ectopic ACTH, adrenal tumor, exogenous | Depression, alcoholism, obesity, severe illness      |
| Key Diagnostic Feature | Fluctuating symptoms and biochemical hypercortisolism      | Persistent symptoms and biochemical hypercortisolism      | Correlation with stress/illness, normal diurnal rhythm |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Cyclic Cushing syndrome is a rare variant characterized by intermittent hypercortisolism. The key diagnostic feature is the presence of normal screening tests during asymptomatic periods, necessitating serial testing during symptomatic episodes. The most common cause is an ACTH-secreting pituitary adenoma exhibiting cyclical activity. Recognizing this pattern is crucial for avoiding misdiagnosis.

---

### Question 117: Autoimmune Adrenalitis: Histopathology of Cortical Lymphocytic Infiltration and Atrophy
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the specific histopathological features of autoimmune adrenalitis (Addison disease), particularly the selective destruction of the adrenal cortex while sparing the adrenal medulla. (问题考察的是自身免疫性肾上腺炎（加生病）的具体组织病理学特征，特别是选择性破坏肾上腺皮质，同时保留肾上腺髓质。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 30-year-old woman with a known history of vitiligo and untreated primary adrenal insufficiency (Addison disease) is found deceased at home. Autopsy reveals bilateral adrenal glands that are markedly atrophic, each weighing 1.2 grams (normal adult weight: 4-5 grams). Histopathological examination of the adrenal glands shows near-total destruction and collapse of the adrenal cortex, characterized by extensive lymphocytic infiltration (predominantly T lymphocytes), plasma cells, macrophages, and fibrous scarring. The adrenal medulla, however, appears structurally normal, with well-preserved chromaffin cells and no evidence of inflammatory infiltration. Laboratory findings prior to death included hyponatremia (125 mEq/L), hyperkalemia (6.0 mEq/L), hypoglycemia (60 mg/dL), and low morning serum cortisol (2 mcg/dL) with inappropriately low ACTH (5 pg/mL, normal range 10-60 pg/mL, indicating primary adrenal failure).

Which anatomical compartment of the adrenal gland is completely spared from autoimmune inflammatory destruction in autoimmune Addison disease?

(A) Zona glomerulosa
(B) Zona fasciculata
(C) Zona reticularis
(D) Adrenal medulla
(E) Adrenal capsule

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 30-year-old woman with vitiligo (autoimmune association) and untreated Addison disease (primary adrenal insufficiency).
- **Autopsy Findings:** Bilateral adrenal glands are markedly atrophic (1.2g each vs normal 4-5g). This indicates significant loss of adrenal tissue.
- **Histopathology:** Near-total destruction and collapse of the *adrenal cortex* with dense lymphocytic infiltration, plasma cells, macrophages, and fibrosis. This points towards an inflammatory process targeting the cortex.
- **Medulla Status:** The *adrenal medulla* is described as structurally normal, well-preserved, and free of inflammatory infiltrates. This is a crucial observation indicating selective sparing.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Hyponatremia, hyperkalemia, hypoglycemia, low cortisol, and low ACTH confirm primary adrenal insufficiency (failure of both glucocorticoid and mineralocorticoid production, leading to aldosterone deficiency and subsequent electrolyte disturbances, plus loss of cortisol production). The low ACTH rules out secondary adrenal insufficiency (pituitary cause).
- **Question Stem:** Asks which anatomical compartment is *completely spared* from the autoimmune destruction.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺皮质生理机制与分子生物学推导)
- **Adrenal Anatomy:** The adrenal gland consists of two distinct parts: the outer cortex and the inner medulla. The cortex is further divided into three zones (glomerulosa, fasciculata, reticularis) responsible for producing mineralocorticoids (aldosterone), glucocorticoids (cortisol), and adrenal androgens, respectively. The medulla produces catecholamines (epinephrine, norepinephrine).
- **Autoimmune Addison Disease:** This is the most common cause of primary adrenal insufficiency in developed countries. It is an autoimmune disorder where the body's immune system attacks the adrenal cortex.
- **Pathogenesis:** The autoimmune attack is primarily directed against enzymes involved in steroidogenesis within the cortical cells. The most common target antigen is 21-hydroxylase (CYP21A2), an enzyme crucial for cortisol and aldosterone synthesis. Antibodies against other enzymes (e.g., 17alpha-hydroxylase, 11beta-hydroxylase, side-chain cleavage enzyme) and adrenal cortex cells themselves can also be present.
- **Histopathology Specificity:** The autoimmune process specifically targets the steroidogenic cells of the adrenal cortex. The inflammatory infiltrate (lymphocytes, plasma cells, macrophages) leads to the destruction of these cells across all three zones (glomerulosa, fasciculata, reticularis). This destruction results in cortical atrophy and fibrosis.
- **Medullary Sparing:** The adrenal medulla, responsible for catecholamine synthesis and derived from neural crest cells (neuroectoderm), expresses different antigens and is not targeted by the autoimmune process in Addison disease. Therefore, the medulla remains structurally and functionally intact. This selective sparing is a hallmark histological feature of autoimmune adrenalitis.
- **Other Causes of Adrenal Insufficiency:** While autoimmune destruction is common, other causes exist (e.g., infections like TB, hemorrhage, metastatic cancer, genetic defects like CAH). However, the clinical presentation (vitiligo association, specific histopathology) strongly points towards autoimmune etiology.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient has Addison disease (primary adrenal insufficiency) confirmed by low cortisol, low ACTH, hyponatremia, and hyperkalemia.
2. The history of vitiligo suggests an autoimmune etiology.
3. Autopsy reveals severely atrophic adrenal glands.
4. Histopathology shows destruction of the adrenal cortex with lymphocytic infiltration and fibrosis.
5. Crucially, the adrenal medulla is described as normal and spared from inflammation.
6. Therefore, the anatomical compartment spared from the autoimmune destruction is the adrenal medulla.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Zona glomerulosa:** This is the outermost zone of the cortex, responsible for aldosterone production. It is affected by the autoimmune destruction in Addison disease, leading to aldosterone deficiency (hyponatremia, hyperkalemia). Incorrect.
- **(B) Zona fasciculata:** This is the middle zone of the cortex, responsible for cortisol production. It is also affected by the autoimmune destruction, leading to cortisol deficiency (hypoglycemia, hypotension, etc.). Incorrect.
- **(C) Zona reticularis:** This is the innermost zone of the cortex, responsible for adrenal androgen production. It is also affected by the autoimmune destruction, although androgen deficiency is less clinically significant than cortisol or aldosterone deficiency. Incorrect.
- **(D) Adrenal medulla:** This is the inner part of the adrenal gland, responsible for catecholamine production. It is derived from neuroectoderm and is specifically spared in autoimmune Addison disease because the autoimmune attack targets cortical steroidogenic enzymes. Correct.
- **(E) Adrenal capsule:** The capsule is the outer connective tissue layer surrounding the cortex. While fibrosis can extend to involve the capsule, the primary site of destruction is the cortical cells themselves, not the capsule as a primary target. More importantly, the medulla is *completely* spared from the inflammatory process, making it the most accurate answer regarding selective sparing. Incorrect.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Feature             | Autoimmune Addison Disease (Primary Adrenal Insufficiency) |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------|
| **Affected Tissue** | Adrenal Cortex (all zones: glomerulosa, fasciculata, reticularis) |
| **Spared Tissue**   | Adrenal Medulla                                            |
| **Pathology**       | Lymphocytic infiltration, cortical atrophy, fibrosis        |
| **Cause**           | Autoimmune attack on steroidogenic enzymes (e.g., 21-hydroxylase) |
| **Hormones Deficient**| Cortisol, Aldosterone, Adrenal Androgens                  |
| **Medulla Function**| Normal (Catecholamine production intact)                   |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Autoimmune Addison disease is characterized by selective autoimmune destruction of the adrenal cortex, leading to deficiency of all cortical steroid hormones. The adrenal medulla, being embryologically distinct (neuroectodermal origin) and expressing different antigens, is characteristically spared from this autoimmune attack. This selective sparing is a key histopathological feature differentiating autoimmune Addison disease from other causes of adrenal destruction.

---

### Question 118: Adrenal Cortex Physiological Mechanism
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system (RAAS), specifically how angiotensin II stimulates aldosterone secretion from the adrenal zona glomerulosa, and how pharmacological inhibition of this pathway (ACE inhibitors, ARBs) leads to hypoaldosteronism and subsequent hyperkalemia.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 66-year-old man with a history of type 2 diabetes mellitus and chronic kidney disease (CKD) stage 3b (eGFR 42 mL/min/1.73m²) is being treated for diabetic nephropathy and proteinuria. His baseline serum potassium is 4.8 mEq/L, and serum creatinine is 1.6 mg/dL. He is started on lisinopril 20 mg daily. Ten days later, routine laboratory tests show his serum potassium has increased to 6.1 mEq/L, and his serum creatinine has increased to 1.9 mg/dL. Plasma renin activity (PRA) is low (1.2 ng/mL/hr, normal range 1.5-4.0), and serum aldosterone is low (3.5 ng/dL, normal range 4.0-12.0). A cosyntropin stimulation test shows an appropriate cortisol response (baseline 15 mcg/dL, 60-minute post-stimulation 25 mcg/dL).

What class of antihypertensive medications is most likely responsible for the patient's hyperkalemia in this clinical context?

(A) Beta-blockers
(B) Calcium channel blockers
(C) Angiotensin-converting enzyme (ACE) inhibitors
(D) Diuretics (thiazide or loop)
(E) Alpha-blockers

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 66-year-old male with type 2 diabetes and CKD (eGFR 42). This background is crucial as CKD often leads to hyporeninemic hypoaldosteronism, making patients more susceptible to hyperkalemia when RAAS is further inhibited.
- **Medication:** Started on lisinopril (an ACE inhibitor). This is the key intervention.
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - **Hyperkalemia:** Serum K+ increased from 4.8 to 6.1 mEq/L. This is the primary clinical problem.
    - **Increased Creatinine:** Serum creatinine increased from 1.6 to 1.9 mg/dL. This is a known side effect of ACE inhibitors, especially in patients with renal artery stenosis or volume depletion, but also can occur due to hemodynamic effects in CKD.
    - **Low PRA and Aldosterone:** PRA is low (1.2 ng/mL/hr) and aldosterone is low (3.5 ng/dL). This pattern is consistent with hypoaldosteronism, likely secondary to RAAS blockade. The baseline low PRA suggests pre-existing hyporeninemic hypoaldosteronism common in diabetic nephropathy.
    - **Normal Cosyntropin Test:** Rules out primary or secondary adrenal insufficiency affecting cortisol production. The adrenal cortex is capable of responding to ACTH stimulation for cortisol synthesis.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the class of medication responsible for hyperkalemia by blocking the primary secretagogue of the adrenal zona glomerulosa.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺皮质生理机制与分子生物学推导)
- **Embryology & Zonation:** The adrenal cortex originates from coelomic mesoderm and is divided into three zones: zona glomerulosa (outermost), zona fasciculata (middle), and zona reticularis (innermost). The zona glomerulosa is responsible for mineralocorticoid (aldosterone) production.
- **Steroidogenesis in Zona Glomerulosa:** Cholesterol is the precursor for all steroid hormones. In the zona glomerulosa, cholesterol is transported into the mitochondria by the StAR protein (stimulated by ACTH, but primarily regulated by Ang II and K+). Inside the mitochondria, CYP11A1 (desmolase) cleaves cholesterol to pregnenolone. Pregnenolone is then converted to progesterone. The key enzyme for aldosterone synthesis is CYP11B2 (aldosterone synthase), which converts progesterone to deoxycorticosterone (DOC) and then DOC to corticosterone, and finally corticosterone to aldosterone.
- **Regulation of Aldosterone Secretion:** The primary regulators of aldosterone secretion are plasma potassium concentration and the renin-angiotensin system (specifically Angiotensin II).
    - **Angiotensin II:** Angiotensin II is the most potent physiological stimulus for aldosterone secretion. It binds to AT1 receptors on zona glomerulosa cells, activating phospholipase C, increasing intracellular calcium levels (via Gq pathway), and stimulating CYP11B2 activity and StAR protein expression.
    - **Potassium:** High plasma potassium directly depolarizes zona glomerulosa cells, opening voltage-gated calcium channels and increasing intracellular calcium, thereby stimulating aldosterone release.
    - **ACTH:** ACTH has a relatively minor role in regulating aldosterone secretion in adults, although it is essential for the development and maintenance of the zona glomerulosa.
- **ACE Inhibitors and Aldosterone:** Lisinopril is an ACE inhibitor. ACE converts Angiotensin I to Angiotensin II. By inhibiting ACE, lisinopril reduces the levels of Angiotensin II. The reduction in Angiotensin II removes the primary stimulus for aldosterone secretion from the zona glomerulosa. This leads to decreased aldosterone synthesis and release (hypoaldosteronism).
- **Consequences of Hypoaldosteronism:** Aldosterone acts on the principal cells of the renal collecting duct. It binds to the mineralocorticoid receptor (MR), leading to increased transcription and insertion of epithelial sodium channels (ENaC) and Na+/K+-ATPase pumps into the apical and basolateral membranes, respectively. It also increases the activity of ROMK channels (potassium channels) in the apical membrane. Reduced aldosterone levels lead to decreased ENaC and Na+/K+-ATPase activity, impairing sodium reabsorption and potassium secretion. This results in decreased renal potassium excretion and subsequent hyperkalemia. Patients with underlying CKD often have reduced renin production (hyporeninemic hypoaldosteronism), making them particularly vulnerable to hyperkalemia when RAAS is pharmacologically inhibited.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient developed hyperkalemia (K+ 6.1 mEq/L) shortly after starting lisinopril.
2. Lisinopril is an ACE inhibitor.
3. ACE inhibitors block the conversion of Angiotensin I to Angiotensin II.
4. Angiotensin II is the primary physiological stimulus for aldosterone secretion from the adrenal zona glomerulosa.
5. Reduced Angiotensin II levels due to ACE inhibition lead to decreased aldosterone secretion (hypoaldosteronism).
6. Aldosterone promotes potassium excretion in the renal collecting duct.
7. Hypoaldosteronism impairs renal potassium excretion, leading to hyperkalemia.
8. The patient's low PRA and aldosterone levels confirm hypoaldosteronism.
9. The normal cosyntropin test confirms the adrenal cortex's ability to produce cortisol, ruling out primary adrenal insufficiency as the cause of hypoaldosteronism.
10. Therefore, the ACE inhibitor (lisinopril) is the most likely cause of the hyperkalemia by blocking the primary secretagogue (Angiotensin II) of the adrenal zona glomerulosa.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Beta-blockers:** Beta-blockers primarily affect the cardiovascular system by blocking beta-adrenergic receptors. Non-selective beta-blockers can slightly inhibit renin release (beta-1 receptors on juxtaglomerular cells), potentially leading to mild decreases in aldosterone, but they do not directly block the primary secretagogue (Angiotensin II) of the zona glomerulosa and rarely cause significant hyperkalemia unless combined with other factors. Cardioselective beta-blockers have minimal effect on renin. This is incorrect.
- **(B) Calcium channel blockers:** Calcium channel blockers (CCBs) primarily affect vascular smooth muscle and cardiac conduction. They do not directly inhibit the RAAS pathway or aldosterone secretion. They are not typically associated with hyperkalemia. This is incorrect.
- **(C) Angiotensin-converting enzyme (ACE) inhibitors:** As detailed above, ACE inhibitors block the formation of Angiotensin II, the primary stimulus for aldosterone secretion. This leads to hypoaldosteronism and impaired renal potassium excretion, causing hyperkalemia, especially in patients with CKD or diabetes. This aligns perfectly with the clinical presentation and laboratory findings. This is the correct answer.
- **(D) Diuretics (thiazide or loop):** Thiazide diuretics inhibit NaCl reabsorption in the distal convoluted tubule, leading to volume depletion, which stimulates renin release and subsequently increases aldosterone levels (secondary hyperaldosteronism). They typically cause hypokalemia, not hyperkalemia. Loop diuretics inhibit NaCl reabsorption in the thick ascending limb of the loop of Henle, also leading to volume depletion and increased renin/aldosterone, and they also increase potassium excretion, causing hypokalemia. This is incorrect.
- **(E) Alpha-blockers:** Alpha-blockers block alpha-adrenergic receptors, primarily causing vasodilation and lowering blood pressure. They do not directly affect the RAAS pathway or aldosterone secretion and are not typically associated with hyperkalemia. This is incorrect.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Medication Class | Mechanism of Action | Effect on RAAS | Effect on Aldosterone | Effect on Potassium | Common Side Effect |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ACE Inhibitors | Block Ang I -> Ang II conversion | Decreases Ang II | Decreases | Increases (Hyperkalemia) | Cough, Angioedema, Hyperkalemia, Increased Creatinine |
| ARBs | Block AT1 receptor | Decreases Ang II effects | Decreases | Increases (Hyperkalemia) | Hyperkalemia, Increased Creatinine |
| Beta-blockers | Block beta-adrenergic receptors | May slightly decrease renin (non-selective) | May slightly decrease | Variable, usually no significant effect | Bradycardia, Fatigue, Bronchospasm (non-selective) |
| CCBs | Block calcium channels | No direct effect | No direct effect | No direct effect | Peripheral edema, Constipation, Headache |
| Thiazide Diuretics | Inhibit NaCl reabsorption (DCT) | Increases renin | Increases | Decreases (Hypokalemia) | Hyponatremia, Hypokalemia, Hypercalcemia, Hyperuricemia |
| Loop Diuretics | Inhibit NaCl reabsorption (TAL) | Increases renin | Increases | Decreases (Hypokalemia) | Hypokalemia, Hypomagnesemia, Ototoxicity, Dehydration |
| Alpha-blockers | Block alpha-adrenergic receptors | No direct effect | No direct effect | No direct effect | Orthostatic hypotension, Dizziness |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Pharmacological inhibition of the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system (RAAS) with ACE inhibitors or ARBs reduces Angiotensin II levels, which is the primary stimulus for aldosterone secretion from the adrenal zona glomerulosa. Reduced aldosterone leads to impaired renal potassium excretion, resulting in hyperkalemia, particularly in patients with underlying conditions like CKD or diabetes that predispose them to hyporeninemic hypoaldosteronism. Understanding the RAAS pathway and the specific effects of different drug classes on aldosterone secretion is crucial for predicting and managing electrolyte abnormalities.

---

### Question 119: Familial Hyperaldosteronism Type 2 and 3: CLCN2 and KCNJ5 Germline Mutations
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the genetic basis and pathophysiology of different subtypes of Familial Hyperaldosteronism (FH), specifically differentiating FH-1 (GRA), FH-2 (CLCN2 mutations), and FH-3 (KCNJ5 mutations) based on their underlying molecular mechanisms and clinical features.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 12-year-old girl presents with severe, refractory hypertension (blood pressure 170/110 mmHg) and hypokalemia (serum potassium 2.8 mEq/L). Her past medical history is unremarkable except for early-onset hypertension diagnosed at age 6. Family history is significant for her mother and maternal uncle, both of whom developed severe hypertension in their 20s. Initial endocrine evaluation reveals elevated plasma aldosterone concentration (PAC) of 45 ng/dL (normal range 3-20 ng/dL) and suppressed plasma renin activity (PRA) of <0.1 ng/mL/hr (normal range 1-6 ng/mL/hr). A dexamethasone suppression test (1 mg overnight) failed to suppress aldosterone secretion. Genetic testing identified an autosomal dominant germline mutation in the CLCN2 gene.

Which form of familial hyperaldosteronism is most consistent with these findings?

(A) Familial Hyperaldosteronism Type 1 (GRA)
(B) Familial Hyperaldosteronism Type 2 (FH-2)
(C) Familial Hyperaldosteronism Type 3 (FH-3)
(D) Primary Hyperaldosteronism due to an adrenal adenoma
(E) Primary Hyperaldosteronism due to bilateral adrenal hyperplasia (idiopathic hyperaldosteronism)

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Severe, refractory hypertension and hypokalemia in a young patient:** Suggests excessive mineralocorticoid activity, specifically aldosterone.
- **Family history of early-onset severe hypertension:** Points towards a genetic or inherited cause.
- **Elevated PAC, suppressed PRA:** Classic biochemical profile of primary hyperaldosteronism (autonomous aldosterone production).
- **Failure to suppress aldosterone with dexamethasone:** Rules out glucocorticoid-remediable aldosteronism (FH-1/GRA), which is ACTH-dependent and suppresses with glucocorticoids.
- **Germline mutation in CLCN2:** Directly identifies the genetic defect responsible for the condition.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺皮质生理机制与分子生物学推导)
- **Adrenal Steroidogenesis & Aldosterone Regulation:** Aldosterone synthesis occurs in the zona glomerulosa cells of the adrenal cortex. It is primarily regulated by the renin-angiotensin system (Ang II stimulates aldosterone synthase, CYP11B2) and plasma potassium levels (high K+ stimulates aldosterone secretion). ACTH has a minor role.
- **Familial Hyperaldosteronism (FH) Subtypes:**
    - **FH-1 (Glucocorticoid-Remediable Aldosteronism, GRA):** Caused by a chimeric gene resulting from unequal crossing over between CYP11B1 and CYP11B2. This leads to ACTH-dependent aldosterone hypersecretion because the chimeric gene is under the control of the ACTH-responsive promoter of CYP11B1. Aldosterone secretion suppresses with glucocorticoid administration (dexamethasone).
    - **FH-2:** Caused by gain-of-function mutations in the CLCN2 gene, which encodes the ClC-2 voltage-gated chloride channel. This channel is constitutively active, leading to increased chloride efflux from zona glomerulosa cells. The resulting membrane depolarization opens voltage-gated calcium channels, increasing intracellular calcium and stimulating aldosterone synthesis and secretion independent of Ang II or K+. This process is *not* ACTH-dependent and does *not* suppress with dexamethasone.
    - **FH-3:** Caused by gain-of-function mutations in the KCNJ5 gene, which encodes the Kir3.4 inwardly rectifying potassium channel. Loss of potassium efflux leads to membrane depolarization, opening voltage-gated calcium channels, and stimulating aldosterone synthesis and secretion. This is also ACTH-independent and does not suppress with dexamethasone. FH-3 typically presents earlier and more severely than FH-2.
- **CLCN2 Gene and ClC-2 Channel:** The CLCN2 gene provides instructions for making the ClC-2 protein, a chloride channel found in various tissues, including the adrenal zona glomerulosa. Mutations in CLCN2 lead to a constitutively open channel, causing excessive chloride efflux and subsequent aldosterone hypersecretion.
- **Distinguishing FH-2 and FH-3:** Both are ACTH-independent and dexamethasone-resistant. FH-3 (KCNJ5 mutation) is generally associated with more severe, earlier-onset hypertension compared to FH-2 (CLCN2 mutation). However, the specific genetic finding (CLCN2 mutation) in the vignette definitively points to FH-2.
- **Other Causes of Primary Hyperaldosteronism:** Adrenal adenoma (Conn syndrome) or bilateral adrenal hyperplasia (idiopathic hyperaldosteronism) are common causes but are typically sporadic, not inherited in an autosomal dominant pattern with specific gene mutations like FH-2 or FH-3.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient presents with hypertension and hypokalemia, suggesting hyperaldosteronism.
2. Elevated aldosterone and suppressed renin confirm primary hyperaldosteronism.
3. The strong family history points towards a genetic etiology.
4. Failure of dexamethasone suppression rules out FH-1 (GRA).
5. The identified germline mutation in the CLCN2 gene directly implicates FH-2.
6. Therefore, the patient's condition is Familial Hyperaldosteronism Type 2.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Familial Hyperaldosteronism Type 1 (GRA):** Incorrect. FH-1 is characterized by ACTH-dependent aldosterone secretion and suppression with dexamethasone. This patient failed dexamethasone suppression.
- **(C) Familial Hyperaldosteronism Type 3 (FH-3):** Incorrect. FH-3 is caused by mutations in the KCNJ5 gene, not CLCN2. While it shares features like ACTH-independence and dexamethasone resistance, the specific genetic finding rules it out.
- **(D) Primary Hyperaldosteronism due to an adrenal adenoma:** Incorrect. Adrenal adenomas are typically sporadic, not inherited in an autosomal dominant pattern with CLCN2 mutations. While they cause primary hyperaldosteronism, the genetic finding points specifically to FH-2.
- **(E) Primary Hyperaldosteronism due to bilateral adrenal hyperplasia (idiopathic hyperaldosteronism):** Incorrect. Idiopathic hyperaldosteronism is also typically sporadic. The strong family history and specific CLCN2 mutation point to a genetic form of hyperaldosteronism, specifically FH-2.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Feature                     | FH-1 (GRA)                  | FH-2 (CLCN2)                 | FH-3 (KCNJ5)                 | Adrenal Adenoma          | Idiopathic Hyperaldosteronism |
| :-------------------------- | :--------------------------- | :--------------------------- | :--------------------------- | :----------------------- | :----------------------------- |
| Inheritance                 | Autosomal Dominant           | Autosomal Dominant           | Autosomal Dominant           | Sporadic                 | Sporadic                       |
| Genetic Cause               | CYP11B1/CYP11B2 Chimerism    | CLCN2 Gain-of-Function        | KCNJ5 Gain-of-Function        | N/A                      | N/A                            |
| ACTH Dependence             | Yes                          | No                           | No                           | No                       | No                             |
| Dexamethasone Suppression   | Yes                          | No                           | No                           | No                       | No                             |
| Typical Onset               | Variable                    | Variable                    | Early Childhood              | Variable                 | Variable                       |
| Severity                    | Variable                    | Variable                    | Often Severe                 | Variable                 | Variable                       |
| Key Mechanism               | ACTH-driven aldosterone      | Cl- efflux -> depolarization | K+ efflux -> depolarization | Autonomous aldosterone | Autonomous aldosterone        |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Familial hyperaldosteronism encompasses several genetic subtypes. FH-1 (GRA) is ACTH-dependent and dexamethasone-suppressible due to a chimeric gene. FH-2 and FH-3 are ACTH-independent and dexamethasone-resistant, caused by gain-of-function mutations in CLCN2 (ClC-2 chloride channel) and KCNJ5 (Kir3.4 potassium channel), respectively, leading to membrane depolarization and autonomous aldosterone secretion. Identifying the specific gene mutation is crucial for accurate diagnosis.

---

### Question 120: Adrenal Cortical Hyperplasia Patterns: Micronodular (Carney) vs Macronodular (BMAH)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating patterns of bilateral ACTH-independent adrenal hyperplasia based on gross pathology, associated genetic syndromes, and clinical features (Carney complex vs. BMAH).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old woman presents with symptoms of Cushing syndrome, including central obesity, moon facies, purple striae, and hypertension. She has no history of exogenous steroid use. Laboratory tests reveal suppressed plasma ACTH (<5 pg/mL) and elevated 24-hour urinary free cortisol (180 mcg/day). Adrenal imaging shows bilateral adrenal enlargement. Due to the ACTH-independent nature of the hypercortisolism, bilateral adrenalectomy is performed. Pathological examination of the adrenal glands reveals two distinct patterns in different patients.

Patient A's glands are normal-sized to slightly small (total weight 10 g), appearing studded with numerous tiny (1-3 mm), dark brown to black nodules. The intervening cortex is markedly atrophic.

Patient B's glands are massively enlarged (total weight 250 g), appearing as large, yellow-orange masses composed of multiple large (2-4 cm) nodules throughout the cortex.
